Presentations of Dehn quandles
Neeraj K. Dhanwani, Hitesh Raundal, Mahender Singh

Abstract
. The paper gives two approaches to write explicit presentations for the class of Dehn quandles using presentations of their underlying groups. The first approach gives finite presentations for Dehn quandles of a class of Garside groups and Gaussian groups. The second approach is for general Dehn quandles when the centralisers of generators of their underlying groups are known. Several examples including Dehn quandles of spherical Artin groups, surface groups and mapping class groups of orientable surfaces are given to illustrate the results.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 20M32, 57K12; Secondary 57K20, 20F36

1.
Introduction

A quandle is an algebraic system with a binary operation that encodes the three Reidemeister moves of planar diagrams of links in the 3-space. Besides being fundamental to knot theory as observed first in
[
22
,
23
,
28
]
, these objects appear in the study of mapping class groups
[
40
]
, set-theoretic solutions of the quantum Yang-Baxter equation and Yetter-Drinfeld Modules
[
12
]
, Riemannian symmetric spaces
[
26
]
and Hopf algebras
[
2
]
, to name a few. Many classical topological, combinatorial and geometric knot invariants such as the knot group
[
22
,
28
]
, the knot coloring, the Conway polynomial, the Alexander polynomial
[
22
]
and the volume of the complement in the 3-sphere of a hyperbolic knot
[
21
]
can be retrieved from the knot quandle. Though quandles give strong invariants of knots, the isomorphism problem for them is hard. This has motivated search for newer properties, constructions and invariants of quandles themselves.

Understanding of presentations of quandles is a fundamental problem and determining a presentation is challenging in general. Even for the simplest quandles arising from groups, such as conjugation quandles of infinite abelian groups, the number of generators and relations turn out to be infinite. In this paper, we give two approaches to write explicit presentations for a fairly large class of quandles called Dehn quandles introduced recently in
[
11
]
. We believe that understanding of presentations has the potential to lead to a combinatorial quandle theory.

The notion of a Dehn quandle of a group is motivated by two classes of examples. The first being the free quandle on a set
\mathchar 29011
, which is simply the union of conjugacy classes of elements of
\mathchar 29011
in the free group on
\mathchar 29011
, equipped with the quandle operation of conjugation. The second class of examples is given by surfaces. Let
\mathcal{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29031}
be the mapping class group of a closed orientable surface
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 29031}
of genus
\mathchar 29031\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28721
and
\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 29031}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}
the set of isotopy classes of non-separating simple closed curves on
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 29031}
. It is known that
\mathcal{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29031}
is generated by Dehn twists along finitely many simple closed curves from
\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 29031}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}

[
13
, Theorem 4.1]
. The binary operation

\mathchar 28939\mathchar 8707\mathchar 28940\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29012_{\mathchar 28940}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28939\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

where
\mathchar 28939\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28940\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 29031}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}
and
\mathchar 29012_{\mathchar 28940}
is the Dehn twist along
\mathchar 28940
, turns
\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 29031}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}
into a quandle called the Dehn quandle of the surface
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 29031}
. It turns out that
\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 29031}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}
can be seen as a subquandle of the conjugation quandle
\operatorname{\mathchar 28995\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29034}\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29031}\delimiter 84054785
of the mapping class group
\mathcal{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29031}
of
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 29031}
, by identifying the isotopy class of a simple closed curve with the isotopy class of the corresponding Dehn twist. These quandles originally appeared in the work of Zablow
[
40
,
41
]
. He derived a homology theory based on Dehn quandles of surfaces
[
42
]
and showed that isomorphism classes of Lefschetz fibrations over a disk correspond to quandle homology classes in dimension two. Further, in
[
6
]
, the Dehn quandle structure of the torus has been extended to a quandle structure on the set of its measured geodesic foliations and the quandle homology of this extended quandle has been explored. On the other hand,
[
25
,
38
,
39
]
considered a quandle structure on the set of isotopy classes of simple closed arcs on an orientable surface with at least two punctures, and called it the quandle of cords. In the case of a disk with
\mathchar 29038
punctures, this is simply the Dehn quandle of the braid group
\mathchar 28994_{\mathchar 29038}
with respect to its standard Artin generators. A presentation for the quandle of cords of the real plane and the 2-sphere has been given in
[
25
]
. Beyond these special cases, we have not seen explicit presentations of Dehn quandles of surfaces in the literature. In fact, other than the well-known procedure of writing presentations of link quandles of tame links via their planar diagrams, general results on presentations of quandles do not seems to be known. However, an analogue of Tietze’s theorem relating two finite presentations of a quandle is known due to
Fenn and Rourke
[
14
, Theorem 4.2]
. Dehn quandles of groups with respect to their subsets include many well-known constructions of quandles from groups including conjugation quandles, free quandles, Coxeter quandles
[
1
]
, Dehn quandles of closed orientable surfaces, quandles of cords of orientable surfaces, knot quandles of prime knots, core quandles of groups and generalized Alexander quandles of groups with respect to fixed-point free automorphisms, to name a few. See
[
11
]
for details.

The paper is organized as follows. We give two approaches to write explicit presentations of Dehn quandles using presentations of their underlying groups. In Section
2
, using Garside theory, we give finite presentations of Dehn quandles of groups of fractions of Garside monoids and Gaussian monoids (Theorem
2.20
and Theorem
2.28
). We give examples of presentations for Dehn quandles using this approach including those of spherical Artin quandles. In Section
3
, we prove a general result giving a presentation of the Dehn quandle of a group with respect to a generating set when the centraliser of each generator is known (Theorem
3.1
). As a consequence, it follows that is
\mathchar 28999\mathchar 12349\delimiter 69632778\mathchar 29011\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29010\delimiter 86414091
is a finitely presented group such that the centraliser of each generator from
\mathchar 29011
is finitely generated, then the Dehn quandle
\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29011^{\mathchar 28999}\delimiter 84054785
is finitely presented (Corollary
3.2
). Although Theorem
3.1
is general, finding generating sets for centralisers of elements in interesting classes of groups like Garside groups and Artin groups is usually challenging. We give presentations for Dehn quandles of surface groups, braid groups and mapping class groups of orientable surfaces to illustrate the result. Examples of braid groups show that presentations of Garside quandles given by Theorem
3.1
usually have larger number of relations than the ones given by Theorem
2.20
.

2.
Presentations of Gaussian quandles and Garside quandles

for basic facts on quandles. Throughout the paper, we consider only right distributive quandles and follow conventions used in

. If

and

. For simplicity of notation, we write such an element as

\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 8707^{\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 28721}}\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8707^{\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 28722}}\mathinner{\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}}\mathchar 8707^{\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 29038}}\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 314

Understanding of presentations of quandles is fundamental to the development of quandle theory. In this section, we give presentations of Dehn quandles of certain Gaussian groups and Garside groups, which we shall refer as
Gaussian quandles
and
Garside quandles
, respectively. The notion of a Gaussian group and a Garside group was first introduced by Dehornoy and Paris
[
10
]
and developed further in the works of Dehornoy
[
9
]
and Picantin
[
31
,
32
,
33
]
. The definition of a Garside group which many authors use first appeared in
[
9
]
. Many equivalent definitions of a Garside group can be seen in the literature. For example, see
[
4
,
8
,
15
,
18
]
. Note that a Garside group is referred as a
small Gaussian group
or a
thin Gaussian group
in
[
10
,
31
,
32
,
33
]
, and the notion of a Garside group in
[
10
]
is a special kind of a Garside group (see the comment after the definition of a Garside monoid). The theory of Garside groups is largely inspired by the work of Garside
[
17
]
in which he treated braid groups, and by the work of Brieskorn and Saito
[
5
]
generalizing the work of Garside to Artin groups of spherical type.

2.1.
Preliminaries on Gaussian and Garside monoids, and groups

An element
\mathchar 29025
of a monoid
\mathchar 29005
is called an
atom
if
\mathchar 29025\mathrel{{{{\mathchar 566\mathchar 61}}}}\mathchar 28721
and
\mathchar 29025
is indecomposable (i.e. if
\mathchar 29025\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29026\mathchar 29027
, then either
\mathchar 29026\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721
or
\mathchar 29027\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721
). A monoid
\mathchar 29005
is called
atomic
if the atoms of
\mathchar 29005
are finite in number, they generate
\mathchar 29005
, and if for every
\mathchar 29048\mathchar 12850\mathchar 29005
, there exists an integer
\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 29048}
such that
\mathchar 29048
cannot be written as a product of more than
\mathchar 29006_{\mathchar 29048}
atoms. Using
[
10
, Proposition 2.1(iii)]
, we can say that every atomic monoid is
conical
, i.e. there are no invertible elements other than the identity element of the monoid. In a monoid
\mathchar 29005
, an element
\mathchar 29048
is a
left divisor
of
\mathchar 29049
, or
\mathchar 29049
is a
right multiple
of
\mathchar 29048
, if there exists an element
\mathchar 29050
satisfying
\mathchar 29049\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29048\mathchar 29050
.
Right divisors
and
left multiples
are defined analogously. We denote by
\mathchar 29048\mathchar 12820_{\mathchar 29004}\mathchar 29049
if
\mathchar 29048
is a left divisor of
\mathchar 29049
, and
\mathchar 29048\mathchar 12820_{\mathchar 29010}\mathchar 29049
if
\mathchar 29048
is a right divisor of
\mathchar 29049
. In an atomic monoid
\mathchar 29005
, the left and right divisibility relations (i.e. the relations
\mathchar 12820_{\mathchar 29004}
and
\mathchar 12820_{\mathchar 29010}
) are respectively left and right invariant partial orders on
\mathchar 29005
(see
[
10
, Proposition 2.3]
).

A
right Gaussian monoid
is a monoid
\mathchar 29005
satisfying the following properties:

•

\mathchar 29005
is atomic;

•

\mathchar 29005
is left and right cancellative;

•

\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29005\mathchar 24891\mathchar 12820_{\mathchar 29004}\delimiter 84054785
is a lattice (i.e. the left g.c.d. and the right l.c.m. exist and they are unique for every
\mathchar 29048\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049\mathchar 12850\mathchar 29005
, see the definitions below).

A
left Gaussian monoid
is defined similarly. A
Gaussian monoid
is both left as well as right Gaussian monoid.
Note that the preceding definition of a right Gaussian monoid is different from that of
[
10
]
. We have placed an extra assumption of right cancellativity to insure that such a monoid embeds in its group of fractions (see
[
7
, Theorem 1.23]
for Ore’s criterion).

For elements
\mathchar 29048
and
\mathchar 29049
in a right Gaussian monoid, the
left greatest common divisor (left g.c.d.)
of
\mathchar 29048
and
\mathchar 29049
is a common left divisor
\mathchar 29050
of
\mathchar 29048
and
\mathchar 29049
such that every common left divisor of
\mathchar 29048
and
\mathchar 29049
is a left divisor of
\mathchar 29050
. The
right least common multiple (right l.c.m.)
of
\mathchar 29048
and
\mathchar 29049
is a common right multiple
\mathchar 29050
of
\mathchar 29048
and
\mathchar 29049
such that every common right multiple of
\mathchar 29048
and
\mathchar 29049
is a right multiple of
\mathchar 29050
. Similarly, in a left Gaussian monoid, we can define the
right g.c.d.
and the
left l.c.m.
of elements.

For elements
\mathchar 29048
and
\mathchar 29049
in a right Gaussian monoid, denote the left g.c.d. and the right l.c.m. of
\mathchar 29048
and
\mathchar 29049
respectively by
\mathchar 29048\mathchar 8798\mathchar 29049
and
\mathchar 29048\mathchar 8799\mathchar 29049
. The
right residue
of
\mathchar 29048
in
\mathchar 29049
(denoted by
\mathchar 29048\delimiter 69657359\mathchar 29049
) is the unique element
\mathchar 29050
satisfying
\mathchar 29048\mathchar 8799\mathchar 29049\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29048\mathchar 29050
. Thus, we have

\mathchar 29048\mathchar 8799\mathchar 29049\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29048\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\delimiter 69657359\mathchar 29049\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29049\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29049\delimiter 69657359\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29049\mathchar 8799\mathchar 29048\mathchar 314

A
Garside element
of a monoid
\mathchar 29005
is an element
\mathchar 28673\mathchar 12850\mathchar 29005
such that the left divisors of
\mathchar 28673
coincide with the right divisors of
\mathchar 28673
, they are finite in number, and they generate
\mathchar 29005
. We say that a Gaussian monoid
\mathchar 29005
is a
Garside monoid
if it contains a Garside element.
By
[
10
, Proposition 2.2]
, the set of atoms in
\mathchar 29005
is contained in every generating subset of
\mathchar 29005
. It follows that atoms are also divisors of
\mathchar 28673
. In
[
10
,
31
,
32
,
33
]
, a Garside monoid is referred as a
small Gaussian monoid
or a
thin Gaussian monoid
. One can look at
[
9
, Proposition 2.1]
for a necessary and sufficient condition on a monoid to be a Garside monoid. Note that the notion of a
Garside monoid
in
[
10
]
is slightly restricted in the sense that the left l.c.m. and the right l.c.m. of atoms in a Garside monoid should coincide and it is a Garside element in the sense defined above.

The
group of fractions
of a monoid
\mathchar 29005
is the group which has the same presentation as that of the monoid
\mathchar 29005
. In other words, if a monoid
\mathchar 29005\mathchar 12349\delimiter 69632778\mathchar 29011\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29010\delimiter 86414091
is the quotient of the free monoid on
\mathchar 29011
modulo the relations in
\mathchar 29010
, then its group of fractions is the quotient of the free group on
\mathchar 29011
modulo the relations in
\mathchar 29010
.

A
Gaussian group
(respectively, a
Garside group
) is the group of fractions of a Gaussian monoid (respectively, of a Garside monoid). Similarly, we can also define a
right Gaussian group
and a
left Gaussian group
.

An
Ore monoid
is one that embeds in its group of fractions. Ore’s criterion says that if a monoid
\mathchar 29005
is left and right cancellative, and if any two elements of
\mathchar 29005
have a common right multiple, then
\mathchar 29005
embeds in its group of fractions
[
7
, Theorem 1.23]
. Note that a Gaussian monoid (respectively, a Garside monoid) satisfies Ore’s conditions, and thus embeds in the corresponding Gaussian group (respectively, in the corresponding Garside group). The same is true for right Gaussian monoids as well as left Gaussian monoids.

Let
\mathchar 29005
be a right Gaussian monoid,
\mathchar 28993
the set of atoms in
\mathchar 29005
and
\mathchar 28999
the group of fractions of
\mathchar 29005
. Then the Dehn quandle
\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28993^{\mathchar 28999}\delimiter 84054785
will be referred as a
right Gaussian quandle
. The terms
left Gaussian quandles
,
Gaussian quandles
and
Garside quandles
are defined similarly. For example, in case of a spherical Artin group, the Artin quandle as defined in
[
11
, Section 3]
is a Garside quandle. Here, note that, by
[
5
]
and
[
10
, Example 1]
, a spherical Artin group is a Garside group.

Let
\mathchar 29005
be a monoid generated by a finite set
\mathchar 29011
and
\mathchar 28999
the group of fractions of
\mathchar 29005
. For words
\mathchar 29048
and

\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29011\mathchar 8795\mathchar 29011^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 8707}

Proposition 2.4

There are many examples of Garside monoids in the literature, for example,
[

,
10
,

,

Proposition 2.10
.

Pairs of types
\mathcal{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 28722}
through
\mathcal{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 28729}
are of type
\mathcal{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 28721}
.

Proof.

For a right Gaussian monoid
\mathchar 29005
, by
[
10
, Proposition 2.2]
, the set of atoms in
\mathchar 29005
is a finite generating set for
\mathchar 29005
. With this in mind, together with Remark
2.1
, Remark
2.2
and Lemma
2.3
, we can conclude that:

•

Pairs of types
\mathcal{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 28724}
and
\mathcal{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 28725}
are of type
\mathcal{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 28721}
.

•

Pairs of types
\mathcal{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 28726}
and
\mathcal{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 28727}
are of type
\mathcal{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 28722}
.

•

Pairs of types
\mathcal{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 28728}
and
\mathcal{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 28729}
are of type
\mathcal{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 28723}
.

Since every Garside monoid is of course a right Gaussian monoid, a pair
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29006\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29012\delimiter 84054785
can be viewed as a pair
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29005\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011\delimiter 84054785
of a right Gaussian monoid with
\mathchar 29005\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29006
and a finite generating set
\mathchar 29011\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29012
for
\mathchar 29005
. Thus, by taking
\mathchar 29005\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29006
,
\mathchar 29011\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29012
,
\mathchar 28953\mathchar 12349\operatorname{\mathchar 29001\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 29006}
(where
\operatorname{\mathchar 29001\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 29006}
denotes the identity map of
\mathchar 29006
), we conclude that a pair of type
\mathcal{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 28723}
is of type
\mathcal{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 28722}
. Now, it is enough to prove that a pair of type
\mathcal{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 28722}
is of type
\mathcal{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 28721}
.

Let
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29005\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011\delimiter 84054785
be a pair of type
\mathcal{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 28722}
. Then, there exist a triple
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29006\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29012\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28673\delimiter 84054785
with
\mathchar 29012\mathchar 28673\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28673\mathchar 29012
and an epimorphism
\mathchar 28953\mathchar 12346\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29006\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29012\delimiter 84054785\twoheadrightarrow\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29005\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011\delimiter 84054785
. In order to prove that the pair
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29005\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011\delimiter 84054785
is of type
\mathcal{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 28721}
, we only have to show that it satisfies condition (
v
). Let
\mathchar 29043\mathchar 12850\mathchar 29011
and
\mathchar 29048\mathchar 12850\mathchar 29005
be any elements. Since
\mathchar 28953
is surjective and it maps
\mathchar 29012
onto
\mathchar 29011
, there exist
\mathchar 29043^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12850\mathchar 29012
and
\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12850\mathchar 29006
such that
\mathchar 28953\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043^{\mathchar 560}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29043
and
\mathchar 28953\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 560}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29048
. Since divisors of
\mathchar 28673
generate
\mathchar 29006
, we can write
\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29028_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 29028_{\mathchar 28722}\mathinner{\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}}\mathchar 29028_{\mathchar 29038}
for some divisors
\mathchar 29028_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29028_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29028_{\mathchar 29038}
of
\mathchar 28673
. Here, note that
\mathchar 29028_{\mathchar 29033}
’s are not necessarily pairwise distinct. Recall that the map
\mathchar 28958

given by
\mathchar 29048\mathrel{\mathchar 567\mathchar 545}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\delimiter 69657359\mathchar 28673\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 69657359\mathchar 28673

\mathchar 28673
. It is easy to see that
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 28673\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28673\mathchar 28958\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028\delimiter 84054785
for a divisor
\mathchar 29028
of
\mathchar 28673
. Since
\mathchar 28958^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785
is a divisor (in particular, a left divisor) of
\mathchar 28673
, we have
\mathchar 28958^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28958^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 69657359\mathchar 28673\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28673
. Let us set
\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28958^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 69657359\mathchar 28673
for
\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29038
. Then, for each

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
\mathchar 29048\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049\mathchar 12850\mathchar 29011^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29011\mathchar 8795\mathchar 29011^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 8707}}

Theorem 2.14
.

\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29005\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011\delimiter 84054785

\mathchar 28999

This proves the lemma.
∎

Proof of Theorem

.

Thus,

∎

2.4.
Examples of presentations of Garside quandles

Theorem
2.20
and Corollary
2.24
can be used to write presentations of certain Garside quandles.

3.
Presentations of Dehn quandles of groups

In this section, we prove a general result giving presentations of Dehn quandles of groups when the centraliser of each generator is known. Although the result is general, determining generating sets for centralisers of elements in interesting classes of groups like Garside groups and Artin groups is usually challenging. See, for example,
[
4
,
15
,
16
,
18
,
20
,
29
,
33
]
for related works. Further, presentations obtained for Garside quandles using Theorem
3.1
usually have larger number of relations than the one given by Theorem
2.20
. We shall see many examples later in this section.

3.1.
Presentations of Dehn quandles

The following theorem gives a presentation of the Dehn quandle of a group
\mathchar 28999
with respect to a generating set
\mathchar 29011
when a generating set for the centraliser of each element in
\mathchar 29011
is known.

We first claim that if

for all

∎

Corollary 3.2
.

If
\mathchar 28999\mathchar 12349\delimiter 69632778\mathchar 29011\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29010\delimiter 86414091
is a finitely presented group such that the centraliser of each generator from
\mathchar 29011
is finitely generated, then the Dehn quandle
\mathchar 28996\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29011^{\mathchar 28999}\delimiter 84054785
is finitely presented.

It follows from
[
16
, Theorem 12]
that the centraliser of an element in a Garside group is finitely generated. Thus, the corollary holds for Garside groups, which includes spherical Artin groups.

Remark 3.3
.

One can reduce the number of relations in Theorem
3.1
as follows:

(1)

Relations of type
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28722\delimiter 84054785
need to be checked only for those generators in
\mathchar 29011
that represent distinct conjugacy classes in
\mathchar 28999
.

(2)

We write
\mathchar 29011\mathchar 12349\mathchar 8820_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29033}
as a disjoint union of sets where
\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29033}
consists of elements that are conjugate to each other. We equip each
\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29033}
with a partial order such that the corresponding poset graph is a tree. Then, for each
\mathchar 29033
, relations of type
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28723\delimiter 84054785
need to be checked only for elements in
\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29033}
that are adjacent in the corresponding poset graph.

3.2.
Examples of presentations of Dehn quandles

As applications of Theorem
3.1
, we give several examples of presentations of Dehn quandles.

Example 3.4
.

Let
\operatorname{\mathchar 28995\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29029}{\delimiter 67273472\mathbb{\mathchar 29018}_{\mathchar 29038}\delimiter 84054785}
be the core quandle of the cyclic group of order
\mathchar 29038
(also called the dihedral quandle of order
\mathchar 29038
). Then, the quandle operation in
\operatorname{\mathchar 28995\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29029}{\delimiter 67273472\mathbb{\mathchar 29018}_{\mathchar 29038}\delimiter 84054785}
is given by
\mathchar 29048\mathchar 8707\mathchar 29049\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29049\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29048\mod\mathchar 29038
. It is shown in
[
11

that the core quandle of any group is a Dehn quandle of some group. Let

.

Conjecture 3.10
.

Next, we consider Dehn quandles of surfaces. Let
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}
be an orientable surface of genus
\mathchar 29031
with
\mathchar 29040
marked points. The
mapping class group

\mathcal{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}
of
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}
is defined as the set of isotopy classes of orientation preserving self-homeomorphisms of
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}
which permute the set of marked points. A simple closed curve
\mathchar 28939
on
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}
is said to be
non-separating
if
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}\mathchar 8814\mathchar 28939
is connected, and called
separating
otherwise. Separating and non-separating simple closed arcs are defined analogously. Let
\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}
be the set of isotopy classes of all non-separating simple closed curves and simple closed arcs in
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}
. For each isotopy class
\mathchar 29049\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}
of a non-separating simple closed curve, let
\mathchar 29012_{\mathchar 29049}
be the isotopy class of the right hand
Dehn twist
along any non-separating simple closed curve representing
\mathchar 29049
. Similarly, if
\mathchar 29049
is the isotopy class of a non-separating simple closed arc, then we define
\mathchar 29000_{\mathchar 29049}
to be the isotopy class of the anti-clockwise
half twist
about the punctures joined by any arc representing
\mathchar 29049
. This defines an injective map

\mathchar 28956\mathchar 12346\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}\mathrel{\mathchar 300\mkern-3.0mu\mathchar 545}\mathcal{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}

by setting

\mathchar 28956\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\begin{cases}\mathchar 29012_{\mathchar 29048}\text{ if $\mathchar 29048$ is the isotopy class of a non-separating simple closed curve,}\\
\mathchar 29000_{\mathchar 29048}\text{ if $\mathchar 29048$ is the isotopy class of a non-separating simple closed arc.}\end{cases}

For
\mathchar 29048\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}
, defining
\mathchar 29048\mathchar 8707\mathchar 29049\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28956\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29049\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84054785
turns
\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}
into a quandle (see
[
11
]
for details), called the Dehn quandle of the surface
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}
. Note that the set of all Dehn twists along non-separating simple closed curves forms one conjugacy class in
\mathcal{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}
, whereas the set of all half twists along simple closed arcs forms another conjugacy class. For each
\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28720
, the group
\mathcal{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}
is generated by finitely many Dehn twists about non-separating simple closed curves and half twists about simple closed arcs
[
13
, Corollary 4.15]
. Thus, if
\mathchar 29011
is such a generating set for
\mathcal{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}
, then
\mathchar 28956\mathchar 12346\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}\mathchar 12833\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29011^{\mathcal{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}}\delimiter 84054785
becomes an isomorphism of quandles since

\mathchar 28956\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\mathchar 8707\mathchar 29049\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28956\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28956\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29049\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28956\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29049\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 28956\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 28956\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29049\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28956\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8707\mathchar 28956\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29049\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

Hence,
\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}
has the structure of the Dehn quandle of the group
\mathcal{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}
with respect to
\mathchar 29011
.

The construction of Dehn quandle of
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}
for
\mathchar 29040\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720
first appeared in the work of Zablow
[
40
,
41
]
. Further,
[
25
,
39
]
considered the quandle structure on the set of isotopy classes of simple closed arcs in
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}
for
\mathchar 29040\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28722
, and called it
quandle of cords
. In general, the quandle of cords is a subquandle of
\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29040}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}
. In the case of a disk with
\mathchar 29038
marked points, this quandle can be identified with the Dehn quandle of the braid group
\mathchar 28994_{\mathchar 29038}
with respect to its standard set of generators, that is, half twists along the cords. The reader may refer to
[
11
, Section 5]
for generalities on Dehn quandles of surfaces, and
[
13
]
for basic facts about mapping class groups.

Remark 3.12
.

) is the presentation of the knot quandle of the trefoil. This recovers the main result of

, Theorem 3.1]

of a quandle

, Theorem 3.2]

(3.3.1)

Note that
\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}
is referred as the quandle of cords of
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726}
in
[

. It is easy to see that commutativity of
\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28721}}\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28722}}\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28723}}\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28724}}\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28725}}\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28725}}\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28724}}\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28723}}\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28722}}\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28721}}
with
\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 29034}}
for

follows from braid relations, and hence the last relation in (

) is needed only for

. The following theorem together with (

) gives a presentation of

Proposition 3.14
.

\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}\mathrel{\lower 0.5pt\vbox{\halign{$\m@th\textstyle\hfil#\hfil$\cr\mathchar 536\crcr\mathchar 61\crcr}}}\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}
.

Proof.

The map
\mathchar 28945\mathchar 12346\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}\mathchar 12833\operatorname{\mathchar 28997\mathchar 29038\delimiter 69640972}\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}\delimiter 84054785
, given by
\mathchar 28945\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 29048}
, induces a quandle homomorphism
\mathchar 28945^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12346\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}\mathchar 12833\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28945\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29011\delimiter 84054785^{\operatorname{\mathchar 28997\mathchar 29038\delimiter 69640972}\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785
, where
\mathchar 29011
is the generating set of
\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}
as in (
3.3.1
). By
[
11
, Section 3.2]
, the map

\mathchar 28680\mathchar 12346\operatorname{\mathchar 28997\mathchar 29038\delimiter 69640972}\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12833\mathcal{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726}

given by
\mathchar 28680\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 29048}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29048
is a surjective group homomorphism, and hence induces a surjective quandle homomorphism
\mathchar 28680^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12346\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28945\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29011\delimiter 84054785^{\operatorname{\mathchar 28997\mathchar 29038\delimiter 69640972}\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12833\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}
. Since
\mathchar 28945^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 28680^{\mathchar 560}
and
\mathchar 28680^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 28945^{\mathchar 560}
are both identity maps,
\mathchar 28680^{\mathchar 560}
is a quandle isomorphism. Now, recall the presentation of
\mathcal{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 28722}
from (
3.2.30
). It follows that we can factorise
\mathchar 28680\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28680_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 28680_{\mathchar 28721}
, where

\mathchar 28680_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12346\operatorname{\mathchar 28997\mathchar 29038\delimiter 69640972}\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12833\mathcal{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 28722}
and
\mathchar 28680_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12346\mathcal{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12833\mathcal{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726}
are surjective group homomorphisms. These maps induce surjective quandle homomorphisms
\mathchar 28680_{\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12346\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28945\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29011\delimiter 84054785^{\operatorname{\mathchar 28997\mathchar 29038\delimiter 69640972}\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12833\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}
and
\mathchar 28680_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12346\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}\mathchar 12833\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}
such that
\mathchar 28680^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28680_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 28680_{\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 560}
. Since
\mathchar 28680^{\mathchar 560}
is an isomorphism, it follows that both
\mathchar 28680_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28680_{\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 560}
are isomorphisms, and hence
\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}\mathrel{\lower 0.5pt\vbox{\halign{$\m@th\textstyle\hfil#\hfil$\cr\mathchar 536\crcr\mathchar 61\crcr}}}\mathcal{\mathchar 28996}_{\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29043}
.
∎

Remark 3.15
.

Note that, the additional braid and far commutativity induced relations in (
3.3.1
) can be recovered using relations from (
3.2.41
).

in (

) is equivalent to the long relation

\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8707\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8707\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28723}\mathchar 8707\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28724}\mathchar 8707\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28725}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28725}\mathchar 8707\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28724}\mathchar 8707\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28723}\mathchar 8707\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8707\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 28721}
in (

).
